    ]

2.3 ANALYZE CR (no consolidation):

    cr_analysis = [CR-001, CR-002, CR-003, CR-004, CR-005, CR-006]
    # Each constraint is distinct; no consolidation

2.4 ANALYZE IR (generally individual):

    ir_analysis = [
      {id: IR-001, consolidate: false},  # REST distinct
      {id: IR-002, consolidate: false},  # SSE distinct
      {id: IR-003, consolidate: false},  # LangGraph distinct
      {id: IR-004, consolidate: false},  # PostgreSQL distinct
      {id: IR-005, consolidate: false},  # Claude distinct
      {id: IR-006, consolidate: false}   # Observability distinct
    ]

TRANSITION → TRANSFORMING
```

---

### Phase 3: Objective Derivation [TRANSFORMING]

**Entry:** Analysis complete

**Procedure:**
```
3.1 DERIVE Capability Objectives:

    CAP = []
    cap_counter = 0
    
    FOR EACH group IN fr_groups:
      cap_counter++
      
      IF group.consolidate:
        # Consolidated objective
        highest_priority = max_priority([r.priority FOR r IN group.requirements])
        cap = {
          id: "CAP-" + pad(cap_counter, 3),
          statement: derive_achievement_statement(group.name),
          category: "capability",
          priority: map_priority(highest_priority),
          success_criteria: {
            definition: derive_consolidated_definition(group),
            verification: {
              method: determine_method(group),
              description: derive_verification_description(group),
              evidence: derive_evidence(group)
            }
          },
          traces_to: {
            requirements: [r.id FOR r IN group.requirements],
            consolidated: true
          },
          component: union([r.component FOR r IN group.requirements]),
          acceptance_criteria: find_matching_crt(group)
        }
      ELSE:
        # Individual objective
        req = group.requirements[0]
        cap = {
          id: "CAP-" + pad(cap_counter, 3),
          statement: transform_to_achievement(req.statement),
          category: "capability",
          priority: map_priority(req.priority),
          success_criteria: {
            definition: derive_definition(req),
            verification: {
              method: determine_method(req),
              description: req.testability.description,
              evidence: derive_evidence(req)
            }
          },
          traces_to: {
            requirements: [req.id],
            consolidated: false
          },
          component: req.component,
          acceptance_criteria: find_matching_crt(req)
        }
      
      CAP.append(cap)

3.2 DERIVE Quality Objectives:

    QUAL = []
    qual_counter = 0
    
    FOR EACH nfr_item IN nfr_analysis:
      qual_counter++
      
      IF is_list(nfr_item.id):  # Consolidated
        reqs = [get_requirement(id) FOR id IN nfr_item.id]
        qual = derive_consolidated_qual(reqs, qual_counter)
      ELSE:
        req = get_requirement(nfr_item.id)
        qual = {
          id: "QUAL-" + pad(qual_counter, 3),
          statement: transform_to_achievement(req.statement),
          category: "quality",
          subcategory: req.subcategory,
          priority: map_priority(req.priority),
          success_criteria: {
            definition: derive_definition(req),
            verification: {
              method: determine_nfr_method(req),
              description: req.testability.description,
              evidence: derive_evidence(req)
            },
            metric: req.metric,
            threshold: derive_threshold(req)
          },
          traces_to: {
            requirements: [req.id],
            consolidated: false
          },
          component: req.component
        }
      
      QUAL.append(qual)

3.3 DERIVE Compliance Objectives:

    COMP = []
    comp_counter = 0
    
    FOR EACH cr IN CR:
      comp_counter++
      comp = {
        id: "COMP-" + pad(comp_counter, 3),
        statement: transform_to_achievement(cr.statement),
        category: "compliance",
        subcategory: cr.subcategory,
        priority: "primary",  # Always primary
        success_criteria: {
          definition: derive_definition(cr),
          verification: {
            method: determine_cr_method(cr),
            description: cr.testability.description,
            evidence: derive_evidence(cr)
          }
        },
        traces_to: {
          requirements: [cr.id],
          consolidated: false
        },
        component: cr.component,
        non_compliance_consequence: cr.violation_consequence
      }
      COMP.append(comp)

3.4 DERIVE Interface Objectives:

    INTF = []
    intf_counter = 0
    
    FOR EACH ir IN IR:
      intf_counter++
      intf = {
        id: "INTF-" + pad(intf_counter, 3),
        statement: transform_to_achievement(ir.statement),
        category: "interface",
        priority: map_priority(ir.priority),
        success_criteria: {
          definition: derive_definition(ir),
          verification: {
            method: "test",
            description: ir.testability.description,
            evidence: derive_evidence(ir)
          }
        },
        traces_to: {
          requirements: [ir.id],
          consolidated: false
        },
        from_component: ir.from_component,
        to_component: ir.to_component,
        protocol: ir.protocol
      }
      INTF.append(intf)

TRANSITION → VALIDATING
```

---

### Phase 4: Validation [VALIDATING]

**Entry:** Objectives derived

**Procedure:**
```
4.1 BUILD success framework:

    all_objectives = CAP + QUAL + COMP + INTF
    
    primary = [o FOR o IN all_objectives WHERE o.priority == "primary"]
    secondary = [o FOR o IN all_objectives WHERE o.priority == "secondary"]
    tertiary = [o FOR o IN all_objectives WHERE o.priority == "tertiary"]
    
    success_framework = {
      minimum_viable: {
        description: "Must achieve for system to be viable",
        criteria: "All primary objectives achieved",
        objectives: [o.id FOR o IN primary],
        objective_count: len(primary),
        requirement_coverage: {
          count: count_requirements(primary),
          ids: get_requirement_ids(primary)
        }
      },
      target: {
        description: "Expected full success state",
        criteria: "All primary and secondary objectives achieved",
        objectives: [o.id FOR o IN primary + secondary],
        objective_count: len(primary) + len(secondary),
        requirement_coverage: {
          count: count_requirements(primary + secondary),
          ids: get_requirement_ids(primary + secondary)
        }
      },
      aspirational: {
        description: "Exceeds expectations",
        criteria: "All objectives achieved",
        objectives: [o.id FOR o IN all_objectives],
        objective_count: len(all_objectives),
        requirement_coverage: {
          count: 46,
          ids: get_all_requirement_ids()
        }
      }
    }

4.2 BUILD traceability:

    # Forward: objective → requirements
    forward = []
    FOR EACH obj IN all_objectives:
      forward.append({
        objective_id: obj.id,
        category: obj.category,
        requirements: obj.traces_to.requirements,
        consolidated: obj.traces_to.consolidated
      })
    
    # Backward: requirement → objective
    backward = {
      functional: [],
      non_functional: [],
      constraint: [],
      interface: []
    }
    
    FOR EACH fr IN FR:
      obj = FIND objective WHERE fr.id IN traces_to.requirements
      backward.functional.append({requirement_id: fr.id, objective_id: obj.id})
    
    # Similar for NFR, CR, IR

4.3 CALCULATE coverage:

    coverage = {
      functional: {
        total: 23,
        covered: count_covered(FR, CAP),
        percentage: (covered / 23) * 100
      },
      non_functional: {
        total: 11,
        covered: count_covered(NFR, QUAL),
        percentage: (covered / 11) * 100
      },
      constraint: {
        total: 6,
        covered: count_covered(CR, COMP),
        percentage: (covered / 6) * 100
      },
      interface: {
        total: 6,
        covered: count_covered(IR, INTF),
        percentage: (covered / 6) * 100
      },
      overall: {
        total: 46,
        covered: sum of above,
        percentage: (covered / 46) * 100
      }
    }

4.4 EXECUTE validation rules:

    errors = []
    warnings = []
    
    # V1-V4: Coverage
    IF coverage.functional.percentage < 100:
      errors.append("V1: Not all FR covered")
    IF coverage.non_functional.percentage < 100:
      errors.append("V2: Not all NFR covered")
    IF coverage.constraint.percentage < 100:
      errors.append("V3: Not all CR covered")
    IF coverage.interface.percentage < 100:
      errors.append("V4: Not all IR covered")
    
    # V5-V6: Success criteria
    FOR EACH obj IN all_objectives:
      IF obj.success_criteria.definition == null:
        errors.append("V5: " + obj.id + " missing definition")
      IF obj.success_criteria.verification.method == null:
        errors.append("V6: " + obj.id + " missing verification")
    
    # V7: COMP priority
    FOR EACH comp IN COMP:
      IF comp.priority != "primary":
        errors.append("V7: " + comp.id + " not primary")
    
    # V8: Traces
    FOR EACH obj IN all_objectives:
      IF len(obj.traces_to.requirements) == 0:
        errors.append("V8: " + obj.id + " has no traces")
    
    # V9: Minimum viable
    IF len(primary) == 0:
      errors.append("V9: No primary objectives")
    
    # V10: Orphans (already checked by V8)
    
    # V11: SARV
    FOR EACH obj IN all_objectives:
      IF NOT is_sarv(obj):
        warnings.append("V11: " + obj.id + " may not be SARV")
    
    # V12: Consolidation preservation
    FOR EACH obj IN all_objectives WHERE consolidated:
      IF NOT all_sources_preserved(obj):
        errors.append("V12: " + obj.id + " missing source IDs")
    
    # V13: Overall coverage
    IF coverage.overall.percentage < 100:
      errors.append("V13: Overall coverage < 100%")

4.5 CALCULATE summary:

    summary = {
      total_objectives: len(CAP) + len(QUAL) + len(COMP) + len(INTF),
      by_category: {
        capability: len(CAP),
        quality: len(QUAL),
        compliance: len(COMP),
        interface: len(INTF)
      },
      by_priority: {
        primary: len(primary),
        secondary: len(secondary),
        tertiary: len(tertiary)
      },
      consolidation: {
        requirements_input: 46,
        objectives_output: len(all_objectives),
        consolidation_ratio: 46 / len(all_objectives),
        consolidated_objectives: count WHERE consolidated == true
      }
    }

4.6 DECISION:

    IF len(errors) > 0:
      LOG errors
      TRANSITION → TRANSFORMING
    ELSE:
      LOG warnings
      TRANSITION → REVIEWING
```

---

### Phase 5: Human Review [REVIEWING]

**Entry:** Validation passed

**Human Interaction:** Required (gate)

**Procedure:**
```
5.1 PREPARE presentation:

    Present Instance1Step3Output:
    
    - Capability Objectives (table: id, statement, priority, traces_to)
    - Quality Objectives (table: id, statement, subcategory, priority, metric)
    - Compliance Objectives (table: id, statement, subcategory, consequence)
    - Interface Objectives (table: id, statement, from, to, protocol)
    
    - Success Framework:
      - Minimum Viable (objectives, requirement coverage)
      - Target (objectives, requirement coverage)
      - Aspirational (objectives, requirement coverage)
    
    - Traceability Summary:
      - Coverage by category
      - Consolidated objectives
    
    - Summary:
      - Total objectives
      - By category
      - By priority
      - Consolidation ratio

5.2 REQUEST decision: APPROVE | REJECT | MODIFY

5.3 WAIT for human response

5.4 PROCESS decision:

    IF APPROVE:
      TRANSITION → COMPLETE
    IF REJECT:
      RECEIVE feedback
      TRANSITION → TRANSFORMING
    IF MODIFY:
      APPLY modifications
      TRANSITION → VALIDATING

Taking a step back now, analyze the salient features of the process we have been instantiating and how it ought to be situation in an AI with human in the loop agency framework for generating output based on problem statement inputs from users.  What is your assessment of the three tiered structure of Instance 0, Instance 1, and Instance N in terms of how knowledge is generated, and how reliable knowledge can be assured.

Jan 1

Architected three-tier knowledge framework balancing autonomy, oversight, and reliability.
54s





































Reflective Analysis: Structured Reasoning as Epistemic Architecture
What We Have Been Instantiating
We have been building an epistemic machine—a system designed to produce reliable knowledge through procedural rigor rather than through trust in AI capability alone. The salient features deserve examination.

1. Externalization of Reasoning
The most radical aspect of this framework is the insistence that reasoning be written down before execution. The four documents (Data Sheet, To Do List, Guidance, Detailed Procedure) are not mere documentation—they are the methodology itself, made inspectable.

This inverts the typical AI interaction pattern:

Typical: Human asks → AI reasons internally → AI produces output
This framework: Human asks → AI produces methodology → AI executes methodology → Human approves output
The methodology becomes a first-class artifact that can be examined, debated, and corrected independently of any particular execution.

2. Separation of Meta-Level and Object-Level
Pass 1 operates at the meta-level: "How should we solve this class of problem?"
Pass 2 operates at the object-level: "Let's actually solve this specific problem."

This separation is philosophically significant. It forces the question: Do we have a good method? before asking Did we get a good answer? When answers are wrong, we can now ask whether the failure was in the method or in the execution.

3. Iterative Coherence (V1→V2→V3)
The three-version iteration with cross-referencing creates internal coherence pressure. Each document must be consistent with the others. The Data Sheet's output contract must match what the Procedure produces. The To Do List must operationalize what the Guidance advises.

This is essentially constraint satisfaction applied to methodology. Inconsistencies surface during iteration, not during execution.

4. Human Judgment at Gates, Not in the Loop
The human role is precisely defined: approve or reject at Pass 2 boundaries. This is different from "human in the loop" where the human collaborates continuously. Here, the human is a gatekeeper, not a co-author.

This has implications:

The AI has autonomy within passes (can work without interruption)
The human has authority at transitions (can halt progress)
The methodology is visible, so the human can evaluate how the answer was reached, not just what the answer is
The Three-Tiered Structure: Epistemological Analysis
Instance 0: The Meta-Methodology
Instance 0 answers the question: What is the structure of good problem-solving?

This is a priori methodological reasoning. We are not solving any particular problem; we are defining what it means to solve problems well. The knowledge produced is:

Abstract (domain-agnostic)
Procedural (describes processes, not facts)
Meta-cognitive (about how to think, not what to think)
Epistemological status: Instance 0 is essentially a theory of knowledge work. Its validity cannot be proven within the system—it is the axiom set from which everything else derives.

Critical question: Who validates Instance 0?

The honest answer is: the same AI that will use it, plus the human who approved the Pass 2 outputs. This is not circular in a vicious sense (all methodologies are created by the entities that use them), but it is a foundation that cannot be more secure than the reasoning that created it.

Instance 1: The Implementation
Instance 1 answers the question: How do we build a system that executes Instance 0?

This is applied methodological reasoning. We take abstract methodology and instantiate it in concrete technology. The knowledge produced is:

Specific (to the technology stack)
Implementational (about how to build, not what to think)
Still procedural (describes software behavior)
Epistemological status: Instance 1 is a translation of Instance 0 into executable form. Its validity depends on two things:

The validity of Instance 0 (inherited)
The correctness of the translation (verified by us)
The translation can introduce errors or improvements. If Instance 1 faithfully implements Instance 0, then Instance 0's strengths and weaknesses are preserved. If Instance 1 deviates (intentionally or not), new validation is needed.

Instance N: The Domain Application
Instance N answers the question: What is true about this specific problem domain?

This is substantive knowledge generation. We are no longer reasoning about methodology—we are using methodology to produce claims about the world. The knowledge produced is:

Domain-specific (about healthcare, finance, engineering, etc.)
Substantive (facts, not just procedures)
Grounded (in the specific problem at hand)
Epistemological status: Instance N is where the framework is tested against reality. The quality of Instance N outputs is the ultimate measure of the entire system. If Instance N produces unreliable knowledge, either:

Instance 0's methodology is flawed, or
Instance 1's implementation is flawed, or
The specific execution had errors, or
The input problem was malformed
The traceability chain lets us diagnose which.

Knowledge Generation: The Flow
Instance 0 (Epistemology)
    │
    │ defines how to think
    ▼
Instance 1 (Implementation)
    │
    │ provides the engine
    ▼
Instance N (Ontology)
    │
    │ produces domain knowledge
    ▼
Outputs (Claims about the world)
This is a top-down knowledge architecture:

Knowledge flows downward through inheritance
Abstract methodology becomes concrete execution
The structure is hierarchical and one-directional
What's missing: There is no upward flow. If Instance N reveals a flaw in Instance 0 (the methodology fails for a class of problems), there is no structured mechanism to propagate that learning back up.

This is a significant limitation. In mature knowledge systems, feedback loops refine the methodology based on outcomes. This framework is open-loop with respect to methodology improvement.

Reliable Knowledge: The Assurance Mechanisms
What Provides Reliability
1. Explicit Methodology You can examine the reasoning before seeing the output. If the methodology is flawed, you can catch it without waiting for a bad output. This is prophylactic quality control.

2. Traceability Every output traces to its source. The chain is:

Objective → Requirement → Stakeholder/Constraint → Problem Statement → User Input
If an output is wrong, you can trace back to find where the error entered. This is auditable reasoning.

3. Human Gates The human sees both the output AND the methodology that produced it. They can reject outputs even if superficially correct, based on methodology concerns. This is deeper than typical AI review.

4. Iterative Coherence The V1→V2→V3 cycle catches internal inconsistencies. Documents that contradict each other get refined. This is self-consistency checking.

5. Validation Rules Explicit validation rules (cardinality checks, coverage requirements, SARV criteria) provide mechanical verification. These catch structural defects.

What Limits Reliability
1. Instance 0 Validity The methodology is only as good as Instance 0. If the 10-step structure is inappropriate for certain problems, all downstream work inherits that flaw. This is the axiomatic risk.

2. Human Attention Quality If humans rubber-stamp approvals (likely after many repetitions), the gates lose their value. The system assumes engaged, competent human review. This is the attention degradation risk.

3. Source Quality Traceability ensures you can trace back to sources, but it doesn't ensure sources are good. "Garbage in, garbage out" still applies. This is the input quality risk.

4. Methodology-Reality Fit The assumption that all problems decompose into the 10-step structure may be false. Some problems may require different structures entirely. This is the procrustean risk.

5. No Learning Loop The system doesn't improve from experience. Instance 100 uses the same Instance 0 methodology as Instance 2. This is the stasis risk.

Situating in AI + Human-in-the-Loop Agency Framework
The Agent Model
This framework implements what might be called procedural agency:

The AI does not reason freely; it follows explicit procedures
The procedures are themselves AI-generated, but then fixed
Autonomy exists within passes; authority exists at gates
This is a middle path between:

Full autonomy (AI decides and acts): Too risky for high-stakes domains
Full human control (human decides everything): Too slow and doesn't leverage AI
The AI has bounded autonomy: it can work independently within the methodology, but cannot proceed without approval.

The Human Model
The human in this framework is:

Approver, not collaborator: They review, they don't co-create
Gatekeeper, not director: They accept/reject, they don't guide
Quality controller: They verify outputs against standards
This is appropriate when:

The human has judgment but not time to do the work themselves
The work is systematic enough to be proceduralizable
The stakes are high enough to warrant approval gates
It may be inappropriate when:

The human lacks expertise to evaluate outputs
Creative collaboration is more valuable than systematic execution
Speed matters more than auditability
Trust Architecture
The framework is built on distrust of AI capability:

Don't trust AI to reason correctly → externalize the methodology
Don't trust AI to execute correctly → require human approval
Don't trust outputs → trace everything back to sources
This is epistemologically conservative. It trades capability for auditability, speed for rigor. It asks: Can we verify this? rather than Is the AI smart enough?

My Assessment
Strengths
Conceptual Clarity: The three-tier structure cleanly separates epistemological concerns. Instance 0 is about how to think, Instance 1 is about how to build, Instance N is about what is true. This is a coherent architecture.

Externalized Reasoning: Making methodology explicit and inspectable is the right move for high-stakes applications. Hidden reasoning is unverifiable reasoning.

Appropriate Distrust: The framework does not assume AI reliability—it creates mechanisms to verify reliability. This is honest about the current state of AI capability.

Traceability as Epistemology: The insistence on tracing every claim to its source is philosophically rigorous. It's essentially implementing "how do you know that?" as a system requirement.

Reusability: A good Instance 0 methodology can be reused across many Instance N applications. The upfront cost amortizes.

Weaknesses
Instance 0 Validation Circularity: The methodology is created by the same AI that will use it, validated primarily by that AI's reasoning. This is the system's epistemological foundation, and it's not independently validated.

No Learning Loop: The framework is open-loop. Instance N outcomes don't refine Instance 0 methodology. Good methodology comes from upfront design, not iterative improvement.

Heavy Overhead: 4 documents × 3 versions × 10 steps = 120 document generations per full instance. For simple problems, this is massive overkill. The framework assumes complex, high-stakes problems.

Human Attention Assumption: The gates only work if humans pay attention. The framework doesn't account for attention degradation over time.

Procrustean Risk: The assumption that all problems fit the 10-step structure may force ill-fitting problems into an inappropriate shape.

Overall
This is a serious framework for serious problems. It's appropriate for:

High-stakes domains where errors are costly
Complex problems where methodology matters
Contexts requiring audit trails and accountability
Situations where human oversight is non-negotiable
It's less appropriate for:

Simple, routine tasks (overhead exceeds value)
Creative or exploratory work (structure constrains emergence)
Time-critical situations (gates slow things down)
Domains where humans can't evaluate AI outputs
The three-tier structure represents a thoughtful approach to the problem of reliable AI-assisted knowledge generation. Its core insight—that methodology should be explicit, inspectable, and separate from execution—is sound. Its limitation—that the meta-methodology itself cannot be externally validated—is honest about the irreducible role of human judgment in epistemology.

The framework doesn't solve the problem of AI reliability; it makes reliability verifiable rather than assumed. That's a meaningful contribution to human-AI collaboration in high-stakes domains.

Generate a prompt that will define a folder structure for this software development project, in light of contemporary best practices and the object-oriented programming structure inherent to this architecture.

Folder Structure Definition Prompt
Context
You are defining the folder structure for a software system that implements a Structured Reasoning Workflow—a methodology-driven AI orchestration platform with human-in-the-loop oversight. The system enables users to execute a 10-step reasoning workflow where an AI agent produces outputs under human supervision.

Technology Stack
Layer	Technology	Purpose
Frontend	Next.js 14+, Vercel AI SDK, assistant-ui	User interface, streaming display
API	FastAPI, Python 3.11+	REST endpoints, SSE streaming
Orchestration	LangGraph 1.0+	State machine, interrupt gates, checkpointing
Persistence	PostgreSQL, pgvector	State, checkpoints, conversations, embeddings
Observability	LangSmith or Langfuse	Tracing, debugging, cost tracking
LLM	Claude API	Agent reasoning
Core Domain Model
The architecture is built on a hierarchical, object-oriented domain model:

Instance Hierarchy
Instance (abstract)
├── Instance0 (Universal methodology)
├── Instance1 (Software implementation) 
└── InstanceN (Domain-specific, N ≥ 2)
Workflow Structure
Workflow
├── Steps (1-10)
│   ├── Pass1 (Definition - autonomous)
│   │   └── Documents
│   │       ├── DataSheet (V1, V2, V3)
│   │       ├── ToDoList (V1, V2, V3)
│   │       ├── Guidance (V1, V2, V3)
│   │       └── DetailedProcedure (V1, V2, V3)
│   └── Pass2 (Execution - supervised)
│       └── Deliverable
└── State
    ├── WorkflowState
    └── StepState
Document Types
DataSheet: Input/output contracts, schemas, validation rules
ToDoList: Phased checklist with validation hooks
Guidance: Context, principles, anti-patterns
DetailedProcedure: State machine, algorithmic execution
Requirement/Objective Categories
Requirements
├── Functional (FR)
├── NonFunctional (NFR)
├── Constraint (CR)
└── Interface (IR)

Objectives
├── Capability (CAP)
├── Quality (QUAL)
├── Compliance (COMP)
└── Interface (INTF)

Separation of Concerns: Clear boundaries between layers (API, orchestration, persistence, domain)
Domain-Driven Design: Core domain logic isolated from infrastructure
Hexagonal Architecture: Ports and adapters pattern for external integrations
Repository Pattern: Abstracted data access
State Machine Pattern: LangGraph workflow as explicit state machine
Event-Driven: SSE streaming, state transitions as events
Configuration-Driven: Environment-based configuration, feature flags

Monorepo Organization
Single repository containing all components
Shared tooling and configuration at root
Clear package/module boundaries
Workspace-based dependency management
Frontend (Next.js)
App Router structure (Next.js 14+)

Clean separation between API layer and domain logic
LangGraph graphs as first-class organizational unit
Repository pattern for data access
Service layer for business logic

Shared Concerns
Type definitions that span frontend/backend
API contracts (OpenAPI, TypeScript types)
Documentation
Infrastructure as code
CI/CD configuration
Constraints

Scalability: Structure should accommodate growth without reorganization
Discoverability: New developers should understand organization quickly
Independence: Layers should be deployable independently where sensible
Convention over Configuration: Follow established conventions for each technology
Deliverable

Provides clear separation between:

Example Domain Objects to Accommodate
python
# These are the key entities the folder structure must support

class Instance:
    id: str
    type: InstanceType  # UNIVERSAL, IMPLEMENTATION, DOMAIN
    parent_instance_id: Optional[str]
    
class Workflow:
    id: str
    instance_id: str
    current_step: int
    current_pass: PassType
    state: WorkflowState
    
class Step:
    number: int  # 1-10
    name: str  # "ProblemStatement", "Requirements", etc.
    
class Pass:
    type: PassType  # DEFINITION, EXECUTION
    
class Document:
    type: DocumentType  # DATA_SHEET, TODO_LIST, GUIDANCE, DETAILED_PROCEDURE
    version: Version  # V1, V2, V3
    content: dict
    
class Requirement:
    id: str  # FR-001, NFR-001, CR-001, IR-001
    category: RequirementCategory
    statement: str
    priority: Priority
    traces_to: List[str]
    
class Objective:
    id: str  # CAP-001, QUAL-001, COMP-001, INTF-001
    category: ObjectiveCategory
    statement: str
    success_criteria: SuccessCriteria
    traces_to: List[str]

class Traceability:
    source_id: str
    target_id: str
    relationship: TraceRelationship

Architecture Specification Review
Document Under Review
2_structured-reasoning-architecture_V1.md

Executive Summary
During Instance 1 implementation (Steps 1-3, Pass 1 and Pass 2), several gaps, ambiguities, and opportunities for improvement became apparent. This review proposes revisions organized by severity and type.

Category 1: Structural Gaps
1.1 Missing Instance 1 in Hierarchy
Current Text (Section 2.1):

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  INSTANCE 0 (Universal)                                         │
│  • Domain-agnostic methodology                                  │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                          ▼ inherits                             │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  INSTANCE N (Domain-Specific)                                   │
│  • Specializes Instance 0 for a specific domain                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Problem: The specification shows a two-tier hierarchy (Instance 0 → Instance N), but the meta-prompt and our implementation use a three-tier hierarchy (Instance 0 → Instance 1 → Instance N). Instance 1 is the software implementation layer that sits between the abstract methodology and domain applications.

Proposed Revision:

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  INSTANCE 0 (Universal Methodology)                             │
│  • Domain-agnostic, abstract                                    │
│  • Defines HOW to do structured reasoning                       │
│  • Created once, inherited by Instance 1                        │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                          ▼ inherits                             │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  INSTANCE 1 (Software Implementation)                           │
│  • Implements Instance 0 as executable software                 │
│  • Adds technology-specific concerns                            │
│  • Created once, used by all Instance N                         │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                          ▼ used by                              │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  INSTANCE N (Domain-Specific, N ≥ 2)                            │
│  • Uses Instance 1 software for specific domains                │
│  • Adds domain-specific categories and validations              │
│  • One instance per problem domain                              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Rationale: This three-tier model emerged as essential during implementation. Instance 1 translates abstract methodology into concrete software—a distinct epistemological level that requires its own specialization pass.

1.2 Missing Interface Requirements Category
Current Text (Section 3.1): The document defines workflows and documents but does not explicitly categorize requirements by type.

Problem: During Step 2 implementation, we found it necessary to introduce four requirement categories:

Functional (FR)
Non-Functional (NFR)
Constraint (CR)
Interface (IR) ← Not mentioned in original spec
Proposed Addition (New Section 3.3: Requirement Categories):

### 3.3 Requirement Categories

Step 2 produces requirements in four categories:

| Category | Prefix | Description | Derived From |
|----------|--------|-------------|--------------|
| Functional | FR | What the system does | Problem statement, stakeholder needs |
| Non-Functional | NFR | Quality attributes | Stakeholder concerns, soft constraints |
| Constraint | CR | Non-negotiable limits | Hard constraints |
| Interface | IR | Component interactions | Integration points |

Each requirement includes:
- Unique identifier (e.g., FR-001)
- Statement ("The system shall...")
- Priority (must/should/could)
- Traceability to source
- Testability criteria
Rationale: The integration points defined in Section 9 naturally generate interface requirements. Making IR explicit ensures integration concerns aren't overlooked.

1.3 Missing Objective Categories
Current Text: No mention of objectives or Step 3 output structure.

Problem: Step 3 transforms requirements into objectives, but the spec doesn't define the objective structure or categories.

Proposed Addition (New Section 3.4: Objective Categories):

### 3.4 Objective Categories

Step 3 produces objectives in four categories:

| Category | Prefix | Description | Derived From |
|----------|--------|-------------|--------------|
| Capability | CAP | What is achieved | Functional requirements |
| Quality | QUAL | Quality levels achieved | Non-functional requirements |
| Compliance | COMP | Constraints satisfied | Constraint requirements |
| Interface | INTF | Integrations working | Interface requirements |

Each objective includes:
- Unique identifier (e.g., CAP-001)
- Achievement statement ("X is achieved")
- Success criteria (definition + verification)
- Priority (primary/secondary/tertiary)
- Traceability to requirements
Rationale: Objectives are the success criteria against which the implementation is measured. Without explicit structure, Step 3 output would be ambiguous.

1.4 Missing Success Framework
Current Text: No concept of tiered success levels.

Problem: During Step 3, we needed to organize objectives into a success framework (Minimum Viable, Target, Aspirational) to map to requirement priorities.

Proposed Addition (New Section 3.5: Success Framework):

### 3.5 Success Framework

Objectives are organized into three levels:

| Level | Includes | Meaning | Maps To |
|-------|----------|---------|---------|
| Minimum Viable | Primary objectives | System fails without | must requirements |
| Target | + Secondary objectives | System disappoints without | + should requirements |
| Aspirational | + Tertiary objectives | System exceeds expectations | + could requirements |

This framework enables:
- Phased delivery (MVP first)
- Clear success/failure criteria
- Prioritized implementation
Rationale: Priority mapping needs a framework to be meaningful. The three-tier success model makes priorities actionable.

Category 2: Clarification Needed
2.1 Document Iteration Dependencies
Current Text (Section 3.2):

V1 (Initial):
Data Sheet V1      ← Step purpose + previous step output
To Do List V1      ← Data Sheet V1
Guidance V1        ← Problem Statement + Data Sheet V1 + To Do List V1
Detailed Proc V1   ← Data Sheet V1 + To Do List V1 + Guidance V1
Problem: The dependency notation is correct but doesn't explain WHY each document considers the others. This led to ambiguity about what "considering" means.

Proposed Revision:

V1 (Initial Creation):

1. DATA SHEET V1
   Input: Step purpose + previous step output contract
   Creates: Input specification, output contract, schemas
   
2. TO DO LIST V1
   Considering: Data Sheet V1
   Creates: Phased tasks to transform input to output
   Why: Tasks must produce what Data Sheet promises
   
3. GUIDANCE V1
   Considering: Problem Statement + Data Sheet V1 + To Do List V1
   Creates: Context, principles, anti-patterns
   Why: Guidance orients to the specific contract and tasks
   
4. DETAILED PROCEDURE V1
   Considering: Data Sheet V1 + To Do List V1 + Guidance V1
   Creates: State machine, algorithmic steps, gates
   Why: Procedure operationalizes all three prior documents
Rationale: Explicit "Why" annotations prevent mechanical document generation without understanding the relationships.

2.2 Human Interaction Protocol
Current Text (Section 5):

| Point | Type | Behavior |
|-------|------|----------|
| Pass 2 step completion | Required gate | Must wait for explicit approval |
Problem: The spec says "must wait for explicit approval" but doesn't specify what the human sees, what actions are available, or how feedback flows back.

Proposed Revision:

### 5.1 Human Review Protocol

At each Pass 2 gate, the system:

1. PRESENTS to human:
   - Step output (the deliverable)
   - Methodology used (V3 documents)
   - Traceability (source → output links)
   - Validation results (errors, warnings)

2. OFFERS actions:
   | Action | Effect | Required Input |
   |--------|--------|----------------|
   | Approve | Advance to next step | None |
   | Reject | Return to execution with feedback | Feedback text |
   | Modify | Apply changes, revalidate | Specific modifications |

3. WAITS indefinitely for human response

4. PROCESSES decision:
   - Approve: Record approval, emit output, advance
   - Reject: Incorporate feedback, re-execute, re-present
   - Modify: Apply changes, validate, re-present
Rationale: Human oversight is the primary assurance mechanism. The protocol must be unambiguous.

2.3 Traceability Scope
Current Text (Section 6.1):

Problem Statement
       │
       ▼ derives
Requirements
       │
       ▼ achieves
Objectives
Problem: This shows the chain but doesn't specify:

What exactly is traced (IDs? Statements? Both?)
Bidirectional vs. unidirectional tracing
Coverage requirements
Proposed Revision:

### 6.1 Traceability Chain

Every output element traces to its source via unique identifiers:
Step 1: Problem Statement
├── Stakeholders (SH-NNN)
├── Hard Constraints (HC-NNN)
├── Soft Constraints (SC-NNN)
└── Success Criteria (CRT-NNN)
│
▼ derives
Step 2: Requirements
├── Functional (FR-NNN) ← SH needs, scope items
├── Non-Functional (NFR-NNN) ← SH concerns, SC
├── Constraint (CR-NNN) ← HC
└── Interface (IR-NNN) ← Integration points
│
▼ achieves
Step 3: Objectives
├── Capability (CAP-NNN) ← FR
├── Quality (QUAL-NNN) ← NFR
├── Compliance (COMP-NNN) ← CR
└── Interface (INTF-NNN) ← IR


Traceability requirements:
- Every output element has traces_to with ≥1 source ID
- Tracing is bidirectional (forward: source→derived; backward: derived→source)
- Coverage: 100% of sources must have ≥1 derived element
Rationale: Traceability is meaningless without ID conventions and coverage requirements.

Category 3: Inconsistencies
3.1 Step Naming Inconsistency
Current Text (Section 2.3):

Step 1:  Problem Statement
Step 2:  Requirements
Step 3:  Objectives
Step 4:  Verification
Step 5:  Validation
Step 6:  Evaluation
Step 7:  Assessment
Step 8:  Implementation
Step 9:  Reflection
Step 10: Resolution
Problem: Steps 4-7 (Verification, Validation, Evaluation, Assessment) sound overlapping. Their distinct purposes are unclear.

Proposed Revision:

Step 1:  Problem Statement    │ Definition Phase
Step 2:  Requirements         │ (What are we solving?)
Step 3:  Objectives           │
─────────────────────────────────────────────────
Step 4:  Verification Design  │ Verification Phase
Step 5:  Validation Design    │ (How will we know it works?)
Step 6:  Evaluation Criteria  │
Step 7:  Assessment Protocol  │
─────────────────────────────────────────────────
Step 8:  Implementation       │ Execution Phase
Step 9:  Reflection           │ (Build and learn)
Step 10: Resolution           │
With definitions:

Step	Question Answered
Verification Design	Does the build match the requirements? (internal)
Validation Design	Does the build solve the problem? (external)
Evaluation Criteria	What metrics define success?
Assessment Protocol	How do we measure and report?
Rationale: Distinct definitions prevent confusion and ensure each step adds value.

3.2 State Model Incomplete
Current Text (Section 4.2):

StepState:
  status: not_started | in_progress | awaiting_review | approved | revision_requested
Problem: Missing states discovered during implementation:

RECEIVED (input received, not yet processing)
ANALYZING (parsing and understanding input)
ELICITING (waiting for clarification from human)
STRUCTURING (building output structure)
VALIDATING (checking output against rules)
Proposed Revision:

StepState:
  # Coarse-grained (for external visibility)
  status: not_started | in_progress | awaiting_review | approved | revision_requested
  
  # Fine-grained (for internal tracking)
  phase: received | analyzing | eliciting | structuring | validating | reviewing | complete
Rationale: Fine-grained phases enable progress visibility and debugging. Coarse-grained status enables simple external queries.

Category 4: Missing Elements
4.1 Clarification/Elicitation Protocol
Current Text (Section 5.1):

| Unclear input | Elicitation | Agent asks clarifying questions |
Problem: No specification of when to elicit, how many questions, what format, or how to proceed without answers.

Proposed Addition (New Section 5.3: Elicitation Protocol):

### 5.3 Elicitation Protocol

When agent encounters insufficient information:

1. SUFFICIENCY CHECK
   - Can the core output be produced?
   - Are critical gaps present?
   - If sufficient: proceed with assumptions documented
   - If insufficient: elicit

2. QUESTION FORMULATION
   - Maximum 5 questions (prefer ≤3)
   - Each question includes:
     - Question text
     - Context (why this matters)
     - Impact if unresolved (what will be assumed)

3. PRESENTATION
   - Questions presented as batch
   - Human may answer all, some, or none
   - Human may direct: "proceed with assumptions"

4. PROCESSING
   - Answered questions: incorporate response
   - Unanswered questions: document assumption with rationale
   - Return to sufficiency check (may need additional round)

5. LIMITS
   - Maximum 3 elicitation rounds per step
   - After limit: proceed with documented assumptions
Rationale: Without explicit protocol, agents may over-elicit (annoying) or under-elicit (produce garbage).

4.2 Validation Rules
Current Text: No mention of validation rules or quality gates.

Problem: During implementation, we defined extensive validation rules (V1-V13). These should be specified.

Proposed Addition (New Section 4.4: Validation Rules):

### 4.4 Validation Rules

Each step has validation rules that must pass before human review:

| Severity | Meaning | Action |
|----------|---------|--------|
| Error | Output is invalid | Must remediate before review |
| Warning | Output may have issues | Note in review, may proceed |

Common validation patterns:
- Cardinality: Element count within range
- Coverage: All sources have derived elements
- Completeness: Required fields present
- Consistency: No contradictions
- Traceability: All outputs trace to sources

Validation executes automatically before REVIEWING state.
Failed validation returns to previous state for remediation.
Rationale: Validation rules are the mechanical quality assurance layer. Without specification, each step invents its own.

4.3 ID Conventions
Current Text: No mention of identifier formats.

Problem: We developed a comprehensive ID system (SH-NNN, HC-NNN, FR-NNN, CAP-NNN, etc.) that should be standardized.

Proposed Addition (New Section 6.3: Identifier Conventions):

### 6.3 Identifier Conventions

All traceable elements use standardized IDs:

| Step | Element | Format | Example |
|------|---------|--------|---------|
| 1 | Stakeholder | SH-NNN | SH-001 |
| 1 | Hard Constraint | HC-NNN | HC-001 |
| 1 | Soft Constraint | SC-NNN | SC-001 |
| 1 | Success Criterion | CRT-NNN | CRT-001 |
| 2 | Functional Req | FR-NNN | FR-001 |
| 2 | Non-Functional Req | NFR-NNN | NFR-001 |
| 2 | Constraint Req | CR-NNN | CR-001 |
| 2 | Interface Req | IR-NNN | IR-001 |
| 3 | Capability Obj | CAP-NNN | CAP-001 |
| 3 | Quality Obj | QUAL-NNN | QUAL-001 |
| 3 | Compliance Obj | COMP-NNN | COMP-001 |
| 3 | Interface Obj | INTF-NNN | INTF-001 |

Rules:
- IDs are unique within their category
- NNN is zero-padded three digits (001, 002, ...)
- Cross-references use full ID (e.g., traces_to: [FR-001, FR-002])
- Instance prefix optional: I1-FR-001 (Instance 1, FR-001)
Rationale: Consistent IDs enable reliable traceability and cross-referencing.

Category 5: Architectural Enhancements
5.1 Add Document Schema Section
Problem: The spec describes document purposes but not their structure.

Proposed Addition (New Section 3.2.1: Document Schemas):

### 3.2.1 Document Schemas

Each document type has a defined structure:

DATA SHEET:
- input_specification: What this step receives
- output_contract: What this step produces
- schemas: Data structures for input/output
- validation_rules: Rules output must satisfy
- state_definitions: States and transitions

TO DO LIST:
- phases: Ordered list of phases
- tasks: Checklist items per phase
- validation_hooks: Checkpoints between phases

GUIDANCE:
- position: Where this step fits in workflow
- principles: Core guidance for execution
- anti_patterns: What to avoid
- quality_criteria: How to assess output

DETAILED PROCEDURE:
- state_machine: States and transitions
- phases: Detailed procedure per phase
- decision_logic: Conditional behavior
- human_protocol: Interaction points
Rationale: Schema definitions prevent document drift and ensure consistency.

5.2 Add Consolidation Concept
Problem: During Step 3, we discovered that multiple requirements often map to single objectives. This "consolidation" concept isn't in the spec.

Proposed Addition (to Section 6: Traceability):

### 6.4 Consolidation

When deriving elements, multiple sources may map to one derived element:

Example:
  FR-001, FR-002, FR-003 (three requirements)
       ↓ consolidate
  CAP-001 (one objective)

Consolidation rules:
- Only consolidate when sources share a single success condition
- Preserve all source IDs in traces_to
- Mark consolidated: true
- Use highest priority among sources
- Do not consolidate if traceability would become obscured

Consolidation ratio = sources / derived elements
Typical range: 1.2 to 2.0 (mild consolidation expected)
Rationale: Without explicit consolidation guidance, Step 3 might produce 1:1 requirement:objective mapping, creating redundancy.

Analysis of Second Colleague's Feedback
This feedback focuses on technical precision for implementation readiness. Let me assess each against V2.1:

Assessment
1. Formalize "Clarification State" in State Model
V2.1 Status: Partially addressed.

V2.1 has:

Fine-grained phase: eliciting in Section 4.2
Phase state machine showing ANALYZING → ELICITING path
Section 5.3 Elicitation Protocol
Gap: The coarse-grained status enum doesn't include a clarification state. When in the eliciting phase, the status is still in_progress, which is ambiguous to external systems (API consumers, frontend).

Recommendation: INCORPORATE - Add awaiting_clarification to the status enum. This makes the state model precise for API consumers who need to know "is the system waiting for user input?"

2. Explicit "Resume" Protocol in Execution
V2.1 Status: Not adequately addressed.

V2.1 Section 8.2 still says:

4. At REVIEWING: LangGraph hits interrupt gate
5. Present output for human review via SSE
6. WAIT for human action (FastAPI endpoint)
7. Resume LangGraph with human input
Gap: This is vague about the actual LangGraph mechanism. The colleague's proposed text is technically precise:

LangGraph raises Interrupt exception (suspends execution)
API returns 202 Accepted with status
Specific POST /resume endpoint
State update mechanism to resume
Recommendation: INCORPORATE - This is essential for implementation. Without it, developers must guess at the interrupt/resume contract.

3. Refine Streaming Contract (SSE Events)
V2.1 Status: Not addressed.

V2.1 Section 9.2 shows "SSE" as the protocol for API→Frontend but doesn't define event types.

Gap: Frontend developers need to know what event types to handle:

Token streaming (for progressive text display)
Status updates (for workflow progress indicators)
Interrupt signals (for gate UI activation)
Recommendation: INCORPORATE - This is a missing interface specification that would block frontend implementation.

4. Instance Seeding with User Context
V2.1 Status: Partially addressed but incomplete.

V2.1 Section 8.1 says:

5. If Instance 1: load Instance 0 V3 methodology as seed
6. If Instance N: load relevant methodology via Instance 1
Gap: This only mentions loading methodology ("how"). It doesn't mention ingesting the user's problem context ("what"). An Instance N workflow needs BOTH to function.

Recommendation: INCORPORATE - This is a logic error. Without the user's problem context, the agent knows how to solve problems but not what problem to solve.

Summary
#	Suggestion	V2.1 Status	Recommendation
1	Clarification state in status enum	Partial (phase only)	Incorporate
2	Explicit resume protocol	Not addressed	Incorporate
3	SSE event types	Not addressed	Incorporate
4	Instance seeding with user context	Logic gap	Incorporate
All four suggestions are valid and should be incorporated. They don't change the conceptual model—they sharpen technical specifications to implementation-ready precision.

Proposed Changes for V2.2
Section	Change
4.2	Add awaiting_clarification to status enum
4.3	Update state transition diagram to show awaiting_clarification
8.1	Expand Instance N initialization to include user problem context
8.2	Replace vague "WAIT" with precise LangGraph interrupt/resume protocol

1. Clarification State:

Status: awaiting_clarification
Phase: eliciting
API Response: 202 Accepted
Endpoint to resume: POST /clarify
2. LangGraph Interrupt/Resume Pattern:

python
# Gate triggers
raise Interrupt(gate_type="review", payload={...})

# Resume via API
await graph.update_state(thread_id, {"human_decision": decision})
await graph.ainvoke(None, config={"thread_id": thread_id})
```

**3. SSE Event Types:**
```
text_delta     → Append tokens to display
status_update  → Update progress indicator  
interrupt      → Show gate UI (clarification or review)
error          → Display error
complete       → Enable next action
```

```
methodology (How) = Instance 0 V3 via Instance 1
problem_context (What) = User's problem description
seed = methodology + problem_context

Jan 1

Weighed specification document value and examined consolidation strategy.








Preliminary Reflection: Value of This Document
Before reviewing the uploaded file, let me address your question about whether this document is valuable given V2.2's comprehensiveness.

The Architecture Spec V2.2 is:

A conceptual specification — describes what the system should be and why
Organized around concepts (state model, traceability, execution protocol, extension mechanism)
Contains rationale, constraints, and design decisions interleaved with specification
Written for stakeholders and designers who need to understand the system holistically
A Technical Spec for an AI Coding Agent needs to be:

An implementation guide — tells how to build it
Organized around components to construct (frontend, API, orchestration, persistence)
Contains actionable specifications stripped of explanatory rationale
Written for developers who need to know what to build, not why
The value of this consolidated spec is:

Extraction — Pulls implementation-relevant details from V2.2 into coder-ready form
Integration — Incorporates the 46 requirements (Step 2) and 34 objectives (Step 3) as acceptance criteria
Organization — Structures by buildable components rather than conceptual domains
Density — An AI agent can consume this as a single, focused prompt without architecture rationale
Think of it as: V2.2 is the "why and what" document; this tech spec is the "what and how" document.

Recommendation: DO NOT INCORPORATE

Executive Summary Highlights
The Problem:

The Structured Reasoning Workflow exists only as abstract specification. There is no executable software that enforces state transitions, manages V1→V3 iteration, or imposes human approval gates.

The Solution:

SOLVER is the concrete software implementation — a Reasoning Engine that orchestrates an LLM through the 10-step workflow with mandatory human validation.

Success Definition:

Human can start workflow with problem statement
System generates Pass 1 methodology (V1→V2→V3)
System generates Pass 2 artifacts with mandatory human gates
Human can approve/reject/revise at each gate
All state survives system restart
Full audit trail is reconstructable
Execution Roadmap Phases
Phase	Goal	Exit Criteria
1: Skeleton	Runnable backend + DB	/health returns 200
2: Orchestration	LangGraph with gates	Graph pauses at awaiting_review
3: API	Full REST + SSE	Interactive flow via curl works
4: Verification	All gates pass	Appendix A tests green
Test Scenario Format (Example: Gate C)
Before (V2.1):

python
async def test_message_does_not_advance():
    workflow = await get_workflow(workflow_id)
    assert workflow.step_state.status == StepStatus.AWAITING_REVIEW
    await send_message(workflow_id, "question")
    workflow = await get_workflow(workflow_id)
    assert workflow.step_state.status == StepStatus.AWAITING_REVIEW

GIVEN a workflow in awaiting_review status
WHEN I send a message via POST /actions/message
THEN:
  - Message is recorded in conversation history
  - Status remains awaiting_review
  - Workflow does NOT advance to next step
Plus the code.

Strategy for Using These Documents with an AI Coding Agent
Document Roles
Document	Role	When to Reference
1_meta-prompt	The "thinking protocol"	When the agent needs to reason about ambiguous requirements or make design decisions
2_architecture	The "why and what"	When the agent asks "why is it designed this way?" or needs conceptual clarity
3_tech-spec	The "how to build"	Primary working document — the agent's implementation guide
Practical Workflow
Phase 0: Project Setup
bash
# Create project structure
mkdir solver && cd solver

# Make docs available to the agent
mkdir -p docs/spec
cp 1_meta-prompt-structured-reasoning.md docs/spec/
cp 2_structured-reasoning-architecture.md docs/spec/
cp 3_solver-technical-spec.md docs/spec/

# Create a condensed "quick reference" the agent can load
cat > docs/spec/AGENT_QUICKSTART.md << 'EOF'
# SOLVER Agent Quick Reference

## What You're Building
SOLVER implements a 10-step, 2-pass reasoning workflow with mandatory human gates.

## Key Files
- `3_solver-technical-spec.md` — YOUR PRIMARY REFERENCE (implementation details)
- `2_structured-reasoning-architecture.md` — Consult for architectural "why" questions
- `1_meta-prompt-structured-reasoning.md` — The methodology being implemented

## Build Order (from Section 15 of tech spec)
1. Phase 1: Skeleton — FastAPI + PostgreSQL + Pydantic models
2. Phase 2: Orchestration — LangGraph state machine + interrupt gates
3. Phase 3: API — REST endpoints + SSE streaming
4. Phase 4: Verification — Run acceptance gate tests A-F

## Critical Constraints
- Pass 2 gates are MANDATORY (cannot advance without human approval)
- `message` action must NOT change workflow state
- All state must survive backend restart
- MVP scope: Steps 1-3 only

## When Stuck
1. Check Section X of tech spec first
2. If architectural question → consult architecture doc
3. If reasoning about requirements → consult meta-prompt
EOF

Recommended Synthesis
The Directive Statement
markdown
# SOLVER Development Directive

## Document Authority Stack (Binding)

You have access to three authoritative documents in `/docs/spec/`:

| Document | Role | Authority |
|----------|------|-----------|
| `1_meta-prompt-structured-reasoning.md` | Operating System | Highest — defines correct reasoning behavior |
| `2_structured-reasoning-architecture.md` | Constitution | Middle — defines system invariants |
| `3_solver-technical-spec.md` | Build Contract | Implementation — defines what to build |

**Conflict Resolution:** If documents conflict, higher authority wins. You may NOT deviate from higher-authority documents to satisfy lower-authority ones.

**Deviation Protocol:** If you believe a deviation is necessary, you must:
1. State which document constraint you're violating
2. Explain why
3. Record in `docs/DECISIONS.md` with timestamp

---

## Operating Protocol (from Doc 1)

- Follow structured reasoning: define scope → propose plan → implement → verify → stop at gates
- No handwaving: no `...` in deliverables. Unknown items become explicit assumptions or open questions.
- Produce small, reviewable increments. Each increment ends with verification.
- If ambiguity blocks work, ask max 3 questions; otherwise proceed with stated assumptions.

---

## Architecture Invariants (from Doc 2)

- Two-pass execution: Pass 1 generates methodology (V1→V2→V3); Pass 2 executes with mandatory human gates
- LangGraph interrupts enforce gates; persistence + checkpoints enable recovery
- Status (external) vs Phase (internal) dual tracking required
- `message` action must NOT change workflow state
- REST + SSE required; PostgreSQL with JSONB artifacts + audit log

---

## Implementation Contract (from Doc 3)

- MVP Scope: Steps 1-3 only (ProblemDefinitionPackage, RequirementsPackage, ObjectivesPackage)
- Meet Acceptance Gates A-F from Appendix A
- Schemas in Section 5 are authoritative
- Endpoints in Section 9 are authoritative
- State model in Section 6 is authoritative

---

## Build Sequence (Gate-Aligned Slices)

| Slice | Deliverable | Gate(s) | Exit Criterion |
|-------|-------------|---------|----------------|
| 1 | Repo skeleton + contracts | A (foundation) | `make lint` passes, schemas exist |
| 2 | Persistence core | D (foundation) | `make migrate` + CRUD tests pass |
| 3 | Orchestration skeleton | C, D | Workflow reaches `awaiting_review` with interrupt |
| 4 | API + SSE | E | Integration test receives SSE events |
| 5 | Step 1-3 generation | B | Artifacts stored, validate against schema |
| 6 | Audit + traceability | F | History endpoint reconstructs timeline |

---

## End-of-Slice Report (Required After Each Slice)
```
Slice: [N]

Changed:
- [file list]

Commands Run:
- [command]: [PASS/FAIL]

Gates:
- Satisfies: Gate [X] because [evidence]
- Not Yet: Gate [Y] because [reason]

Assumptions Made:
- [list any assumptions]

Next Slice:
- [description]
```

---

## Verification Standard

**"If it can't be verified by a command, it's not done."**

Required scripts:
- `make lint` — Code quality
- `make test` — Unit tests
- `make db-up` — Start database
- `make migrate` — Run migrations
- `make e2e` — End-to-end tests
- `python tools/validate_artifact.py` — Schema validation

---

## When You Need More Context

If a section reference is insufficient, load the specific section:
```bash
# Load database schema section
sed -n '/^## 7\. Database/,/^## 8\./p' docs/spec/3_solver-technical-spec.md

# Load state machine section
sed -n '/^## 6\. State Machine/,/^## 7\./p' docs/spec/3_solver-technical-spec.md
```

---

## File Boundaries

- `/docs/spec/` — Read-only (the three authority documents)
- `/docs/DECISIONS.md` — Append-only (deviation log)
- `/apps/`, `/packages/`, `/infra/`, `/tests/` — Your working directories

Final Directive Statement (Synthesized from All Three)
markdown
# SOLVER Development Directive

## Your Role

I am the **Architect**. You are the **Senior Developer** building SOLVER.

You will implement exactly what the documents specify. You may ask clarifying questions (max 3 per task). If blocked, state assumptions explicitly and proceed.

---

## Document Authority Stack

Three documents in `/docs/spec/` govern this project:

| Document | Role | Use It For |
|----------|------|------------|
| `1_meta-prompt-structured-reasoning.md` | **The Why** | Understanding business logic, workflow rules, gate requirements |
| `2_structured-reasoning-architecture.md` | **The Where** | Deciding where logic belongs, system structure, component boundaries |
| `3_solver-technical-spec.md` | **The What** | Exact schemas, table names, file paths, API endpoints, acceptance criteria |

### Authority Rules

- **Behavioral constraints** (gates, state transitions, workflow rules): Doc 1 and Doc 2 win
- **Implementation details** (schemas, types, file paths, table names): Doc 3 wins — use exactly as specified
- **Deviations**: If you must deviate, state which document you're contradicting and log in `docs/DECISIONS.md`

---

## Operating Protocol

From Doc 1 (binding):
- No handwaving: no `...` in deliverables
- Unknown items become explicit assumptions or open questions
- Stop at gates for my approval before proceeding
- Small, reviewable increments only

---

## Architecture Invariants

From Doc 2 (binding):
- Two-pass execution: Pass 1 (methodology V1→V2→V3), Pass 2 (mandatory human gates)
- LangGraph interrupts enforce gates
- `message` action must NOT change workflow state
- Status (external) vs Phase (internal) tracking required
- PostgreSQL + LangGraph checkpointing for recovery

---

## Build Phases and Slices

### Phase A: Scaffolding
| Slice | Task | Doc Reference | Exit |
|-------|------|---------------|------|
| A1 | Directory structure | Doc 3, Section 11 | `tree` matches spec |
| A2 | Contract schemas | Doc 3, Section 5 | JSON schemas exist |
| A3 | Docker + config | Doc 3, Sections 12-13 | `docker-compose up` works |

### Phase B: Persistence
| Slice | Task | Doc Reference | Exit |
|-------|------|---------------|------|
| B1 | Database migrations | Doc 3, Section 7 | `make migrate` passes |
| B2 | Repository classes | Doc 3, Section 7 | CRUD unit tests pass |

### Phase C: Orchestration
| Slice | Task | Doc Reference | Exit |
|-------|------|---------------|------|
| C1 | State models | Doc 3, Section 8.1 | Models match spec |
| C2 | Graph definition | Doc 3, Section 8.2 + Doc 1 (Two-Pass logic) | Graph compiles |
| C3 | Node implementations | Doc 3, Section 8.4 | Workflow reaches `awaiting_review` |

### Phase D: API
| Slice | Task | Doc Reference | Exit |
|-------|------|---------------|------|
| D1 | REST endpoints | Doc 3, Section 9 | Endpoints return correct responses |
| D2 | SSE streaming | Doc 3, Section 9.2 | Events stream correctly |
| D3 | Wire to orchestration | Doc 2, Section 8 | Full flow works |

### Phase E: Verification
| Slice | Task | Doc Reference | Exit |
|-------|------|---------------|------|
| E1 | Gate A-F tests | Doc 3, Appendix A | All gates pass |

---

## Task Execution Protocol

For each slice:

1. **I give you a task** with document section references
2. **You output a plan** — what files you'll create/modify
3. **I approve** (or adjust)
4. **You implement** — do NOT generate code for future slices
5. **You run verification** — commands, not narrative
6. **You report** using this template:
```
Slice: [ID]

Files Changed:
- [path]: [description]

Commands Run:
- `[command]`: [PASS/FAIL]

Gates Satisfied:
- Gate [X]: [evidence]

Gates Not Yet Satisfied:
- Gate [Y]: [reason]

Assumptions Made:
- [list]

Ready for Next Slice: [Yes/No]
```

7. **I approve** → you proceed to next slice

---

## Verification Standard

"If it can't be verified by a command, it's not done."

Required scripts (you create these):
```bash
make lint        # Code quality
make test        # Unit tests  
make db-up       # Start database
make migrate     # Run migrations
make e2e         # End-to-end tests
```

---

If you need more detail than provided, load specific sections:
```bash
# Example: Load database schema section only
sed -n '/^## 7\. Database/,/^## 8\./p' docs/spec/3_solver-technical-spec.md
```

- `/apps/`, `/packages/`, `/infra/`, `/tests/` — Your working directories

Here's some feedback from a colleague of mine.  Consider what has merit and recommend what should be incorporated.

Proposed Revisions
While excellent, I recommend a few minor tactical refinements to make it even more robust for an automated agent:
1. Explicit "Context Loading" Commands: In Section 11, the sed commands are good, but AI agents often struggle with line numbers that change.
   * Revision: Suggest adding a directive to "Read the Table of Contents first" or use a tool like grep to find section headers dynamically if sed ranges fail.
2. The "Meta-Prompt" Injection: The directive mentions 1_meta-prompt-structured-reasoning.md as "The Why."
   * Revision: Explicitly instruct the agent to read Doc 1 before generating any "reasoning" logic (Phase E). Agents often rush to code without internalizing the "Why."
3. Environment Variable Handling: The "Hard Constraints" (Section 4) should explicitly mention .env handling.
   * Revision: Add a constraint: "Secrets must NEVER be hardcoded. All config must load from pydantic_settings reading .env."

Detailed Assessment
1. Context Loading Commands

The colleague is correct. sed -n '/^## 7/,/^## 8/p' assumes:

Section headers don't change
No nested headers match the pattern
Document structure remains stable
More robust approach:

bash
# Find section, then extract
grep -n "^## " doc.md                           # List all sections first
grep -A 200 "^## 7\. Database" doc.md | head -n 150  # Extract ~150 lines after header
Verdict: INCORPORATE with improved commands + "read ToC first" guidance

2. Meta-Prompt Injection Before Phase E

This is an excellent catch. Phase E (Content Generation) is where the agent writes:

LLM prompts for Steps 1-3
Methodology generation logic
The "reasoning behavior" of SOLVER
Without deeply understanding Doc 1's structured reasoning methodology, the agent will generate generic prompts that don't embody:

V1→V2→V3 iteration
Four-document methodology pattern
Gate semantics
Traceability requirements
Verdict: INCORPORATE as explicit Phase E precondition

3. Environment Variable Handling

The tech spec (§12) already shows pydantic_settings with .env, but it's not stated as a constraint. Making it explicit prevents:

Hardcoded API keys in commits
Config drift between environments
Security audit failures
Verdict: INCORPORATE in §4 Hard Constraints

Recommended Changes

No hardcoded secrets	Best practice	All config via pydantic_settings from .env; grep -r "sk-" src/ returns nothing

## 11. Context Loading

If you need more detail than the task provides:

```bash
grep "^## " docs/spec/3_solver-technical-spec.md
```

**Step 2: Extract specific section** (using grep + head, more robust than sed)
```bash
# Database section (~150 lines)

# State machine section
grep -A 100 "^## 6\. State Machine" docs/spec/3_solver-technical-spec.md

# Artifact schemas
grep -A 200 "^## 5\. Artifact Schemas" docs/spec/3_solver-technical-spec.md
```

**Step 3: If section is large**, read subsections:
```bash
grep "^### " docs/spec/3_solver-technical-spec.md | grep -A1 "7\."
```

**Rule:** When in doubt, list sections first (`grep "^## "`), then extract. Do not guess line numbers.

Pass 1 (methodology)	Generate V1→V2→V3 docs	Tech spec V2→V2.1→V2.2
Pass 2 (execution)	Produce artifact with gates	Directive v1.0→v1.1→v1.2

Automated validation	Manual review	No validate_schemas.py on our outputs
These gaps suggest: the process works even when partially applied, but formal structure would add rigor.

The Recursive Insight
We were simultaneously:

Designing Instance 0 (the universal methodology)
Instantiating Instance 1 (SOLVER software specs)
Executing an instance of structured reasoning (our conversation)
The process described itself through its own application. This is the hallmark of a coherent methodology—it can be used to design itself.

Principle	Validated By

Our conversation has been a live proof-of-concept for the methodology we're encoding. The process emerged organically because it reflects how rigorous collaborative work actually happens:

Iterate with feedback
Don't advance without approval
Show your reasoning
Track where things came from
SOLVER isn't inventing a new process—it's formalizing and enforcing the discipline that produces quality outcomes. Our chat is evidence that the discipline works.

Mapped SOLVER framework against orchestration layer requirements.






Analysis: SOLVER as Agentic Orchestration Layer
What an Orchestration Layer Must Do
Function	Description	Risk If Missing
State Management	Track where the agent is, what it's produced	Agent loses context, repeats work, contradicts itself
Task Sequencing	Ensure work happens in correct order	Dependencies violated, incoherent outputs
Gate Enforcement	Physically prevent unauthorized advancement	Agent runs away, produces unchecked outputs
Persistence	Survive crashes, enable resume	Work lost, no recovery
Observability	Know what the agent did and why	Black box, no debugging, no trust
Constraint Enforcement	Prevent prohibited behaviors	Agent violates boundaries
Context Provision	Give agent right information at right time	Hallucination, drift, inconsistency
SOLVER Maps Directly to These Functions
Orchestration Function	SOLVER Component	Implementation
State Management	WorkflowState + StepState	Status (external) + Phase (internal) dual tracking
Task Sequencing	10-step workflow, Pass 1→Pass 2	LangGraph edges enforce order
Gate Enforcement	interrupt() + awaiting_review	Graph physically pauses; cannot resume without human action
Persistence	PostgreSQL + LangGraph checkpoints	State survives restart (Gate D)
Observability	Audit log + traceability links	Every transition recorded with actor, timestamp, artifact
Constraint Enforcement	Hard constraints in orchestration	Pass 2 gates mandatory, message doesn't unpause
Context Provision	Methodology docs + artifact schemas	Agent receives V3 methodology before executing
The Two Layers of Orchestration
What we've built has two orchestration layers:

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                 SOFT ORCHESTRATION LAYER                    │
│                  (Development Directive)                    │
│                                                             │
│  • Rules the agent follows voluntarily                      │
│  • Enforced by: prompt engineering, cultural compliance     │
│  • Failure mode: agent ignores rules, drifts                │
│  • Recovery: human notices, corrects                        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                            ↓
                    Hardens into
                            ↓
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                 HARD ORCHESTRATION LAYER                    │
│                       (SOLVER)                              │
│                                                             │
│  • Rules enforced by infrastructure                         │
│  • Enforced by: LangGraph interrupts, DB constraints        │
│  • Failure mode: system rejects invalid transitions         │
│  • Recovery: automatic (state persisted, gates hold)        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
The Development Directive is the soft layer. It relies on the AI coding agent choosing to comply.

SOLVER is the hard layer. It makes non-compliance physically impossible (can't advance without approval, state persisted regardless of crashes).

Our Conversation Revealed What Needs Hardening
Soft Behavior (Our Chat)	Hard Enforcement (SOLVER)
I waited for your approval	interrupt() pauses graph until approve action
I showed my reasoning	Audit log captures rationale; trace links mandatory
I iterated V1→V2→V3	Workflow enforces version sequence
I didn't skip ahead	Graph edges prevent step N+1 before step N approved
You could reject and request revision	revise action loops back to structuring phase
Changes traced to sources	traceability_links table enforces provenance
The soft layer (our conversation) demonstrated the desired behaviors. The hard layer (SOLVER) makes them guaranteed behaviors.

The Orchestration Insight: Trust Budget
Agentic systems have a trust budget:

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                      TRUST SPECTRUM                         │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                             │
│  FULL AUTONOMY                              FULL CONTROL    │
│       ←───────────────────────────────────────────→         │
│                                                             │
│  Agent acts freely          │          Human approves       │
│  High throughput            │          every action         │
│  High risk                  │          Low throughput       │
│                             │          Low risk             │
│                                                             │
│                      SOLVER SITS HERE                       │
│                            ↓                                │
│              ┌─────────────────────────┐                    │
│              │  Pass 1: More autonomy  │                    │
│              │  Pass 2: Gates required │                    │
│              └─────────────────────────┘                    │
│                                                             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
SOLVER's key insight: different phases deserve different trust levels.

Pass 1 (methodology generation): Configurable gates—can be autonomous or supervised
Pass 2 (artifact production): Mandatory gates—always supervised
This is the orchestration pattern: modulate trust by phase.

What Our Conversation Proved About Orchestration
Observation	Orchestration Principle
You gave approvals ~8 times	Gates should be at natural decision points, not every action
Colleague feedback improved output	External input should be injectable at defined points
V1→V2→V3 produced better artifacts	Iteration loops are a feature, not overhead
I halted when asked	Agents need explicit stop mechanisms
Traceability enabled trust	Provenance is required for human confidence
You never lost context	Persistence is non-negotiable for multi-turn work
The Meta-Pattern: Orchestration for Orchestration
Here's the recursive structure:

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  LEVEL 3: You orchestrating me (our conversation)          │
│           └→ Development Directive as soft constraints     │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  LEVEL 2: SOLVER orchestrating an LLM agent                │
│           └→ LangGraph + gates as hard constraints         │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  LEVEL 1: The LLM reasoning through steps                  │
│           └→ Methodology docs as reasoning scaffolding     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
You're building a generalizable orchestration pattern:

Define the process (Instance 0 / meta-prompt)
Implement the enforcer (SOLVER / LangGraph)
Operate with directives (Development Directive / prompts)
This same pattern works at any level—orchestrating coding agents, orchestrating reasoning agents, orchestrating multi-agent systems.

Key Orchestration Properties You've Encoded
Property	Where Encoded	Why It Matters
Idempotent resume	LangGraph checkpoints	Can restart without corruption
Audit trail	audit_log table	Reconstruct what happened
Explicit state	Status + Phase enums	No hidden state, fully inspectable
Bounded iteration	V1→V2→V3 (three versions)	Prevents infinite loops
Typed artifacts	YAML package schemas	Outputs are validated, not arbitrary
Gate semantics	message doesn't unpause	Actions have defined effects
Context injection	Methodology docs provided to agent	Agent has right information
What's Generalizable Beyond SOLVER
The orchestration layer you're building could be parameterized:

yaml
OrchestrationConfig:
  workflow:
    steps: [list of step definitions]
    passes: [definition, execution]
  
  gates:
    pass_1_policy: none | per_step | end_of_pass
    pass_2_policy: always_required
  
  actions:
    approve: {effect: advance, requires: human}
    revise: {effect: loop_back, requires: human}
    message: {effect: none, requires: any}
  
  artifacts:
    schemas: [paths to JSON schemas]
    validation: required | optional
  
  persistence:
    checkpointing: required
    audit_log: required
  
  trust_levels:
    autonomous_phases: [pass_1]
    supervised_phases: [pass_2]
SOLVER is one instantiation of this general orchestration pattern. The same infrastructure could orchestrate:

Research agents (literature review → synthesis → writing)
Code agents (design → implement → test → deploy)
Analysis agents (data gathering → analysis → reporting)
Conclusion
You're not just building SOLVER. You're building the pattern for supervised agentic orchestration:

Define process as explicit steps with typed outputs
Enforce gates where trust is insufficient
Persist state so work survives failures
Log everything so humans can audit
Inject context so agents have right information
Bound iteration so processes terminate
Our conversation was a prototype run of this pattern. SOLVER hardens it into infrastructure. The Development Directive bridges them—soft rules that become hard enforcement.

The insight: orchestration is the product, not just the means to build it.

2. Staleness Propagation (Critical Gap)

If Step 1 changes materially, Step 2/3 are potentially stale.

This is not in our current design. We have:

Automatic staleness detection

Failure Mode	SOLVER Mitigation	Design Rationale

  stale: boolean
  stale_reason: string | null  # "upstream_changed", "manual_invalidation"
Behavior:

When Step 1 artifact is revised → Mark Step 2, 3 artifacts as stale: true
Stale artifacts require re-review before workflow can complete
Optional: require re-execution, not just re-review

Based on the provided specifications, the **Orchestration Layer** is the "brain" of the SOLVER system, and it is explicitly designed to move beyond simple "chatbot" loops into rigorous, long-running state machines. Here is an analysis of your Orchestration Layer strategy, evaluated against contempo
